Time-varying.\n    39→- Trial outcome. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   111→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   112→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   113→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   114→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   115→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   116→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   117→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   118→\n   119→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   120→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   121→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   122→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   123→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   124→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   125→\n   126→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   127→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   128→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   129→\n   130→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   131→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   132→\n   133→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   134→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   135→\n   136→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   137→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   138→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   139→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   140→\n   141→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   142→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   143→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   144→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   145→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   146→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   147→\n   148→## Decoder Reference\n   149→\n   150→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   151→- Optional arguments:\n   152→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   153→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   154→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   155→\n   156→### Purpose\n   157→\n   158→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   159→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   160→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   161→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   162→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   163→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   164→\n   165→### Consistency requirements\n   166→\n   167→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   168→- Loading of data\n   169→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   170→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   171→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   172→\n   173→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n","numLines":189,"startLine":1,"totalLines":189}}}

The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). Aborted trials, auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded.\n    54→\n    55→ii.\n    56→```python\n    57→trials_table = ref_ds.trials\n    58→...\n    59→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    60→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    61→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    62→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    63→]\n    64→\n    65→for _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n    66→    start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n    67→    end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n    68→    if end_idx > T:\n    69→        end_idx = T\n    70→    if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    71→        continue\n    72→    idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n    73→```\n    74→\n    75→iii. The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n    76→\n    77→## 1-e. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n    80→\n    81→ii.\n    82→```python\n    83→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    84→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    85→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    86→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    87→]\n    88→...\n    89→if end_idx > T:\n    90→    end_idx = T\n    91→if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    92→    continue\n    93→...\n    94→if len(trials) < 2:\n    95→    continue\n    96→```\n    97→\n    98→iii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n    99→\n   100→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n   101→\n   102→i. The Allen SDK provides it pre-computed with neuropil correction and baseline normalization already applied.\n   112→\n   113→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n   114→\n   115→i. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n   116→\n   117→ii.\n   118→```python\n   119→dff_list = []\n   120→plane_labels = []\n   121→for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n   122→    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n   123→    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n   124→    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n   125→    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\n   126→neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n   127→```\n   128→\n   129→iii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n   130→\n   131→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   132→\n   133→i. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n   134→\n   135→ii. N/A\n   136→\n   137→iii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n   138→\n   139→## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   140→\n   141→i. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n   153→\n   154→## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   155→\n   156→i. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). The time bin size is computed from the median inter-frame interval of `ophys_timestamps`.\n   157→\n   158→ii.\n   159→```python\n   160→first_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']\n   161→time_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n   162→```\n   163→\n   164→iii. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n   167→\n   168→i. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n   169→\n   170→ii.\n   171→```python\n   172→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   173→image_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\n   174→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   175→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   176→```\n   177→\n   178→iii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n   179→\n   180→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n   181→\n   182→i. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n   183→\n   184→ii.\n   185→```python\n   186→all_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\n   187→image_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n   188→...\n   189→image_row = np.array(\n   190→    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n   191→    dtype=np.int8)\n   192→```\n   193→\n   194→iii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n   195→\n   196→## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n   197→\n   198→i. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. The switch point is determined by `np.searchsorted` on `change_time`.\n   199→\n   200→ii.\n   201→```python\n   202→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   203→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   204→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   205→```\n   206→\n   207→iii. This is aligned to the neural data because both use the same ophys frame indices.\n   208→\n   209→## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n   210→\n   211→i. Image change is a binary time-varying variable derived from `change_time` and the `go` column in the trials table. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. For catch trials (sham change), `image_change` is 0 throughout.\n   212→\n   213→ii.\n   214→```python\n   215→image_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\n   216→if row['go']:\n   217→    change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n   218→    image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n   219→```\n   220→\n   221→iii. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Catch trials are excluded because no actual image change occurs — the `go` column distinguishes real changes from sham changes.\n   222→\n   223→## 4-b. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n   253→\n   254→ii.\n   255→```python\n   256→f_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n   257→                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   258→running_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n   259→...\n   260→all_running = np.concatenate([\n   261→    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n   262→    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n   263→])\n   264→run_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n   265→...\n   266→run_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n   267→```\n   268→\n   269→iii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n   270→\n   271→## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n   272→\n   273→i. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. By interpolating running speed onto `ophys_ts` upfront, alignment is guaranteed — both neural and running data are indexed by the same ophys frame indices. Based on the AllenSDK code, the clock used for different data stream are synced at the hardware level, so we can safely interpolate.\n   287→\n   288→## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Pupil diameter* derived from?\n   289→\n   290→i. Pupil diameter is derived from `dataset.eye_tracking`, using the `pupil_width` column. Blink frames (where `likely_blink` is True) are excluded before interpolation.\n   291→\n   292→ii.\n   293→```python\n   294→eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n   295→eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n   296→```\n   297→\n   298→iii. `pupil_width` was used as the measure of pupil diameter. Blink frames were removed prior to interpolation to avoid corrupting the signal with blink artifacts. The SDK's `likely_blink` flag provides a pre-computed blink detector.\n   299→\n   300→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n   301→\n   302→i. Pupil diameter is linearly interpolated (after blink removal) from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values are mapped to bin 0.\n   303→\n   304→ii.\n   305→```python\n   306→f_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n   307→                   eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n   308→                   kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   309→pupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n   310→...\n   311→all_pupil = np.concatenate([\n   312→    np.concatenate([t['pupil'] for t in trials])\n   313→    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n   314→])\n   315→pupil_edges = discretize(all_pupil, N_LEVELS)\n   316→...\n   317→pup_disc = apply_discretize(t['pupil'], pupil_edges)\n   318→```\n   319→\n   320→iii. Same approach as running speed: linear interpolation to the ophys timebase, then global percentile-based discretization. Blink removal before interpolation prevents blink artifacts from propagating into neighboring timepoints.\n   321→\n   322→## 6-d. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n   323→\n   324→i. Same approach as running speed — pupil diameter is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data.\n   325→\n   326→ii.\n   327→```python\n   328→eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n   329→eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n   330→f_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n   331→                    eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n   332→                    kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   333→pupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n   334→...\n   335→'pupil': pupil_diameter[idx].astype(np.float32),\n   336→'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n   337→```\n   338→\n   339→iii. Same as running speed — the eye tracking timestamps are hardware-synced with the ophys clock, so interpolation is valid. Using the same `idx` array guarantees alignment.\n   340→\n   341→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n   342→\n   343→i. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Subjects (mice) and sessions are discovered from the Allen SDK's `VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache`, filtered to the `VisualBehavior` project code. All data are pulled using the `get_ophys_experiment_table()` method. For each mouse, all ophys sessions are loaded via `get_behavior_ophys_experiment()`. Data are loaded using the `allensdk` library's S3 cache.\n\nii. Finding all data:\n```python\nbc = bpc.VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache.from_s3_cache(cache_dir=cache_dir)\nexperiment_table = bc.get_ophys_experiment_table()\nvb_experiments = experiment_table[experiment_table.project_code == PROJECT_CODE]\nall_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n```\n\nLoading a session's experiments:\n```python\nfor exp_id in session_experiments.index:\n    datasets[exp_id] = bc.get_behavior_ophys_experiment(exp_id)\n```\n\niii. Each experiment corresponds to one imaging plane in one session; sessions are reconsturcted by combining `experiments` are grouped by the same `ophys_session_id`.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Subjects correspond to unique `mouse_id` values in the experiment table.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n```\n\niii. The `mouse_id` field is the SDK's unique identifier for each animal. The number of mice can be cross-checked against the project documentation.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each session corresponds to a unique `ophys_session_id` in the experiment table. A single session may contain multiple experiments (imaging planes), which are grouped together by matching `ophys_session_id`. Sessions for each mouse are sorted by `date_of_acquisition`.\n\nii.\n```python\nmouse_exps = vb_experiments[vb_experiments.mouse_id == mouse_id]\nmouse_sessions = mouse_exps.drop_duplicates(subset='ophys_session_id')[\n    ['ophys_session_id', 'session_type', 'date_of_acquisition']\n].sort_values(by='date_of_acquisition')\nsession_ids = mouse_sessions['ophys_session_id'].values\n...\nsess_exps = mouse_exps[mouse_exps.ophys_session_id == sid]\n```\n\niii. The `ophys_session_id` groups all imaging planes recorded simultaneously in one behavioral session. Sorting by acquisition date preserves the chronological order of sessions within each mouse.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are defined using the built-in `dataset.trials` table from the Allen SDK. Each trial corresponds to one stimulus change event (go or catch). The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). Aborted trials, auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials_table = ref_ds.trials\n...\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n\nfor _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n    start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n    end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n    if end_idx > T:\n        end_idx = T\n    if end_idx <= start_idx:\n        continue\n    idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n```\n\niii. The SDK's built-in trials table was used, since it provides pre-computed trial metadata (outcome, image identity, timing). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Aborted trials (early lick before change), auto-rewarded trials (free reward), and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded. Trials where `end_idx <= start_idx` (empty window) are skipped. If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n...\nif end_idx > T:\n    end_idx = T\nif end_idx <= start_idx:\n    continue\n...\nif len(trials) < 2:\n    continue\n```\n\niii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `dff_traces` (dF/F calcium fluorescence traces) from each experiment in the session, accessed via `dataset.dff_traces.dff`.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\nneural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n```\n\niii. dF/F is the standard measure of neural activity for two-photon calcium imaging. The Allen SDK provides it pre-computed with neuropil correction and baseline normalization already applied.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n\nii.\n```python\ndff_list = []\nplane_labels = []\nfor exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\nneural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n```\n\niii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). The ophys frames from `start_time` to `stop_time` are extracted, giving a variable-length window per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nstart_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\nend_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\nidx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n...\n'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n```\n\niii. `np.searchsorted` finds the first ophys frame at or after each boundary time. Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). The time bin size is computed from the median inter-frame interval of `ophys_timestamps`.\n\nii.\n```python\nfirst_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']\ntime_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n```\n\niii. The ophys timestamps are already at a consistent frame rate determined by the microscope scanning. No resampling is needed since all data (neural, running, pupil) are aligned to the same ophys timebase.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n\ni. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n\nii.\n```python\nchange_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\nimage_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\nimage_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\nimage_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n```\n\niii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n\ni. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n\nii.\n```python\nall_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\nimage_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n...\nimage_row = np.array(\n    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n    dtype=np.int8)\n```\n\niii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n\n## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. The switch point is determined by `np.searchsorted` on `change_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nchange_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\nimage_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\nimage_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n```\n\niii. The image identity at each frame is determined by whether that frame falls before or after `change_time`. This is aligned to the neural data because both use the same ophys frame indices.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n\ni. Image change is a binary time-varying variable derived from `change_time` and the `go` column in the trials table. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. For catch trials (sham change), `image_change` is 0 throughout.\n\nii.\n```python\nimage_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\nif row['go']:\n    change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n    image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n```\n\niii. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Catch trials are excluded because no actual image change occurs — the `go` column distinguishes real changes from sham changes.\n\n## 4-b. N/A\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Image change* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same as image name — computed per ophys frame using the same `idx` array and `change_time` alignment.\n\nii. The `running_speed` attribute is the SDK's standard interface for locomotion data.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nf_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\nrunning_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n...\nall_running = np.concatenate([\n    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n])\nrun_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n...\nrun_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n```\n\niii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. The same `idx` array is used to extract both.\n\nii.\n```python\nrun = ref_ds.running_speed\nf_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\nrunning_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n...\n'running': running_speed[idx].astype(np.float32),\n'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n```\n\niii. By interpolating running speed onto `ophys_ts` upfront, alignment is guaranteed — both neural and running data are indexed by the same ophys frame indices. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n\ni. Pupil diameter is linearly interpolated (after blink removal) from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. These four columns are the SDK's canonical trial outcome labels for the change detection task. They are mutually exclusive for non-aborted, non-auto-rewarded trials. Only small metadata (`ophys_ts`, `plane_labels`) is retained for later use. The trial-level slices (already copied during `segment_trials`) are the only data kept.\n\nii.\n```python\nsession_meta = {\n    'ophys_ts': session_data['ophys_ts'],\n    'plane_labels': session_data['plane_labels'],\n}\ndel session_data\nsession_results.append((mouse_id, session_meta, trials))\n```\n\niii. The full-session `neural_data` array `(N_neurons, T)` is the largest object in memory. After `segment_trials` copies the per-trial slices, the full array is redundant. bytes\\\\ndata/visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0/behavior_ophys_experiments/behavior_ophys_experiment_8842183\\u202612083 tokens truncated\\u2026pplying the derived spatial transform to segmented cell masks.\\\\n3. For each pair of sessions, solving the linear assignment problem (i.e. bipartite graph matching) to determine which pairs of masks are most likely to be related.\\\\n4. A graph combination method was used to join all the bipartite graphs to determine the most likely label sets that match across all sessions.\\\\n\\\\nDetermining the spatial transformation\\\\nThe module first used an intensity-based method to register the average intensity projection images of each pair of sessions, producing a Euclidean transformation that registered each image pair. We found that a single image registration strategy did not work for all cases. In some cases, the true lateral shifts between two experiments were too large for the ECC registration algorithm. In other cases, the border in the projection images biased the registration towards matching borders and not cell soma. In still other experiments, a histogram equalization step that helped most experiments resulted in a degraded registration.\\\\n\\\\nWe implemented a \\\"meta-registration\\\" where 4 different registration sequences were attempted. Each sequence employed an initial cropping step to eliminate the motion border problem. The sequences employed varying combinations of ECC, PhaseCorrelation, and contrast adjustment. After each of the 4 sequences were attempted, we chose the best candidate based on the structural similarity metric (SSIM) between the two registered images. If none of the candidate sequences improved SSIM beyond that achieved with unregistered images, we flagged the registration as a failure for QC inspection.\\\\n\\\\nIn a few cases, there were still some unexplained failures to register. In these cases, we found that the average projection intensity images had low contrast, but the maximum projection intensity images had more visible features. By substituting in the maximum projections for the registration step, we achieved good registration and good cell matching for these containers.\\\\n\\\\nApplying the spatial transformation to cell masks\\\\nThe cell masks were materialized into a set of images, such that no ROIs overlapped in any one image. We then applied the relative transformation found from the intensity projections to these materialized images, to get the cell masks in an aligned space.\\\\n\\\\nSolving the linear assignment problem between 2 sessions\\\\nTo map cells, a bipartite graph matching algorithm (the Blossom method) was used to find correspondence of cells between sessions. The algorithm used cell labels in the pair-wise experiments as nodes, and the edges of the graph were weighted with weights, w:\\\\nw = d/d_max + (1.0 \\u2212 I/U)\\\\nwhere d is the distance between the 2 mask centroids (in pixels), d_max = 10 pixels, I is the number of pixels that are shared between the 2 masks (the intersection), and U is the total number of pixels covered by the two masks combined (the union). The graph edge for any pair of masks with d > 10 pixels was assigned a very large weight, eliminating that pair as a candidate match.\\\\n\\\\nThe original version of this module used the \\\"Hungarian\\\" method to minimize the total weight. SciPy has an equivalent implementation of that method. We found that the solution from the \\\"Blossom\\\" method was more robust to order permutations of the inputs. We used the networkx implementation of the \\\"Blossom\\\" method. As this implementation seeks to maximize total weight, we inverted the weights, taking care to prevent division-by-zero:\\\\nw = 1 / (1 + w)\\\\n\\\\nBy maximizing the summed weights of edges, the bipartite matching algorithm found the \\\"best match\\\" between cells in each pair of experiments.\\\\n\\\\nJoining the pairwise bipartite graphs\\\\nAll the pairwise bipartite graphs were naively combined. This combined graph is not constrained to avoid labeling conflicts.\\\\n\\\\nMatching was performed on the full set of ROIs identified by the segmentation algorithm, but ROIs that were deemed invalid by the ROI filtering step were not included in the final set of matched cells. For quality control checks, we produced standard plots of pairwise registrations across sessions (Figure 12), as well as quantification of the fraction of ROIs matched across sessions (Figure 13). Session pairs with visible failures to register (clear features in the image were not aligned), or with very low fraction of matched cells (<10% matching between session pairs), were manually evaluated and an attempt was made to optimize the registration process to produce accurate registration. Session pairs that were not possible to register were excluded from the dataset.\\\\n\\\\nFrom \\\"Behavioral strategy shapes activation of the Vip-Sst disinhibitory circuit in visual cortex\\\"\\\\n\\\\nWe used the Allen Institute Visual Behavior\\u20142p calcium imaging dataset to examine how the activity of Vip, Sst, and excitatory cells depends on strategy during a change detection task.41,42 This dataset contains behavior from 376 imaging sessions from 82 mice, thus permitting analysis of behavioral diversity. We used a dynamic logistic regression model7 to identify the strategies used by mice. We find individual mice have stable mixtures of a visual comparison strategy and a statistical timing strategy. Vip, Sst, and excitatory cells recorded from mice predominantly using each strategy show dramatic differences in activity. Further, the effects of strategy were independent from task engagement and stimulus novelty. Finally, we show that strategy differences can be succinctly described by the degree of activation of the Vip-Sst disinhibitory circuit.\\\\n\\\\nWe analyzed the Allen Institute Visual Behavior\\u20142p calcium imaging dataset,42 which contains 2-photon calcium imaging recordings from transgenic mice expressing the calcium indicator GCaMP6f in excitatory neurons (Slc17a7-IRES2-Cre;Camk2a-tTa;Ai93), Sst inhibitory neurons (Sst-IRES-Cre;Ai148), and Vip inhibitory neurons (Vip-IRES-Cre;Ai148). The data were collected using standardized data collection and processing.41,43 During imaging, head-fixed mice performed a visual change detection task (Figure 1A). Mice were shown a series of natural images (250 ms stimulus duration) interspersed with periods of a gray screen (500 ms inter-stimulus duration). This task uses a roving baseline paradigm, whereby an individual image repeats a variable number of times before a new image is presented and then itself repeats. Mice were given a water reward for licking in response to image changes. Premature licking delayed the time of the next image change. To serve as distractors, 5% of image repeats were omitted and replaced with a continuation of the gray screen for the same duration as image presentations. Image changes as well as the image immediately before the change were not omitted. Mice performed the task on a running disk. Running was \\u201copen loop\\u201d with respect to task progression and mice were free to run or not run without influence on stimulus or reward generation. Mice typically ran between licking bouts and stopped running during licking and reward consumption (see Figure S16). Mice learned the task through a standardized training pipeline and were selected for imaging based on a consistent criteria of minimum task performance.\\\\n\\\\nTwo-photon calcium imaging was performed in transgenic mice expressing the calcium indicator GCaMP6f in specific cell populations: excitatory neurons, Sst inhibitory neurons, and Vip inhibitory neurons. Recent work proposed a taxonomy in which excitatory neurons and GABAergic neurons are classes, and Sst and Vip neurons are subclasses, of GABAergic neurons.46 For simplicity, we refer to excitatory, Sst, and Vip as cell classes. The dataset contains recordings collected while mice performed the task with both familiar and novel stimuli41; we restricted our analysis to familiar stimuli (see Figures S20 and S21).\\\\nOur dataset contains 8,619 excitatory cells (21 imaging sessions, 9 mice), 470 Sst cells (15 imaging sessions, 6 mice), and 1,239 Vip cells (21 imaging sessions, 9 mice). These cell classes are thought to form a microcircuit whereby Sst and Vip reciprocally inhibit each other (Figure 4B). We performed our analyses on discrete calcium events that were regressed from the raw fluorescence traces, thus removing the slow decay dynamics of the calcium indicator GCaMP6f (Figure 4C). The extracted calcium events are of variable magnitude and correspond to a transient increase in internal calcium levels.\\\\n\\\\nData selection\\\\nThe collection and processing of all data in the study was previously described in Garrett et al41 and is available at https://portal.brain-map.org/explore/circuits/visual-behavior-2p. For our behavioral analysis we used all active behavioral sessions from mice in the V1 and LM datasets across all image set experience levels (familiar images, novel images, and repeated exposure to novel images). For all analyses we combined mice trained on image sets A and B. For neural analysis we used neurons recorded during familiar image set presentations on the multi-plane imaging rig. Except where noted we combined across cells from V1 and LM and across cortical depths.\\\\n\\\\nMouse training\\\\nAs described in Garrett et al.41 the training pipeline consisted of 7 stages before imaging:\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nTraining 0 - Mice learned to lick for rewards\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nTraining 1 - Mice earned rewards when a static grating changed orientation\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nTraining 2 - Static gratings were interleaved with a gray screen\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nTraining 3 - Static gratings are replaced with natural images\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nTraining 4 - Rewards decrease in size, and free rewards are no longer given when the mouse misses 10 changes in a row\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nTraining 5 - Performance must be consistently above a minimum threshold\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nHabitation - Mice performed the task on the imaging rig\\\\n\\u2022\\\\nImaging - Mice performed the task with familiar images, then with novel images. Imaging was also performed during passive viewing of the same stimulus, which was not analyzed here.\\\\nOur main figures analyze the mouse behavior during the imaging sessions. See Figure S7 for mouse behavior over training.\\\\n\\\\nBehavioral data processing\\\\nWe performed all of our behavioral analysis after assigning behavioral events to each image presentation interval. By image presentation interval we refer to the 750 ms interval beginning with each image presentation. For image omissions we used the 750 ms following the time of the omission, when the image should have been presented. Licks were segmented into licking bouts using an inter-lick interval of 700 ms. This threshold was determined by visual inspection of the histogram of inter-lick intervals. The start and end of each licking bout was then assigned to an image presentation interval.\\\\n\\\\nNeural data\\\\nFor all analysis of neural data we used the detected calcium events as described in Garrett et al.41 and https://portal.brain-map.org/explore/circuits/visual-behavior-2p. This process produces, for each cell, a set of calcium events each with a time and magnitude. Except where noted we used familiar image set sessions collected on the multiplane calcium imaging rig.\\\\n\\\\nDecoding analysis\\\\nTo decode task signals on an image by image basis we used a random forest classifier to predict either image changes versus repeats (change decoder), or hits versus misses (hit decoder). We iterated over the number, n, of simultaneously recorded neurons used in the decoding analysis. For each imaging plane we sampled neurons and performed decoding until there was a 99% probability all neurons had been used in decoding. For each sample we took n neurons without replacement and concatenated their neural activity on each image presentation to make a k x nt matrix X. Here k is the number of images, and t is the number of timesteps from each neuron on each image. The change decoder used each image change and the image repeat immediately before the image change. The hit decoder used all image changes. We then performed 5-fold cross validated decoding using the RandomForestClassifier package from sklearn.58 We evaluated decoder performance as the percentage of test-set images correctly classified. We averaged the performance of all samples from the same imaging plane and report summary statistics as the mean +/- SEM over imaging planes. We computed the correlation between the change decoder\\u2019s predictions and the animal\\u2019s choices using the phi coefficient.\\\\n\\\\n# Setup\\\\nimport numpy as np\\\\nimport Briefly, in this go/no-go task, mice are presented with a continuous series of briefly presented stimuli and they earn water rewards by correctly reporting when the identity of the image changes. Mice undergo standardized training in the behavior facility prior to imaging, in which they first learn to detect orientation changes using static grating stimuli, then transition to flashed gratings, and subsequently learn to detect changes in the identity of natural scene images. Each session included 8 images, for a total of 64 possible transitions. The images used in this study were a selected subset of the natural scenes stimulus set used in the Visual Coding Allen Brain Observatory.\\\\n10:Once mice are well-trained in the task, they transition to performing the task while being imaged with a 2-photon microscope to enable simultaneous measurement of neural activity and behavior. During the imaging portion of the experiment, mice perform the task with the set of eight natural scene images they viewed during training, as well as a novel set of eight images that they had not seen before, in separate sessions. This allows evaluation of the impact of novelty on neural coding for stimulus and behavioral information. Mice also undergo passive viewing sessions during the imaging stage, where the mice are given their daily water before the session (and are thus satiated) and view the task stimuli with the lick spout retracted so they are unable to earn water rewards. Lastly, during imaging sessions only, stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability, disrupting the expected cadence of flashed image presentations. Stimulus changes and the stimulus immediately preceding the change were never omitted.\\\\n13:To ensure that any observed effects of stimulus novelty are due to lack of experience with those images, rather than properties of the specific images that were used, we trained some mice with the opposite stimulus configuration such that the training set for these mice was the image set that was novel for a different group of mice (compare dataset variants VisualBehavior and VisualBehaviorTask1B in Figure 5). This control was performed using the single-plane 2-photon imaging system.\\\\n16:Due to our experimental design, involving repeated imaging of the same population of cells over multiple days, the use of both single- and multi-plane imaging systems, and our strict quality control (QC) criteria that result in failures & retakes of specific session types, the complete 2-photon dataset for a given mouse can take many forms. The terminology described below is used to reference different subsets of the dataset, and these definitions apply to terms used in the AllenSDK, associated data manifest tables, and contents of NWB files.\\\\n18:The data collected in a single continuous recording is defined as a session. For single-plane imaging experiments, there is only one imaging plane (referred to as an experiment) per session. For multi-plane imaging experiments, there can be up to 8 imaging planes (8 experiments) per session. Due to our QC process, not all multi-plane imaging sessions have exactly 8 experiments, as some imaging planes did not meet our QC criteria. We aimed to track the activity of single neurons across multiple session types (familiar and novel image sets, passive and active behavior sessions; described in the overview) by targeting the same population of neurons over multiple recording sessions, with only one session recorded per day for a given mouse. The collection of imaging sessions for a given population of cells, belonging to a single imaging plane measured across days, is called a container. A container can include between 3 and 11 separate sessions for a given imaging plane. Accordingly, the session types available for a given container (corresponding to a specific imaging plane) can vary. Mice imaged with the multi-plane 2-photon microscope can have multiple containers, one for each of the 8 imaging planes recorded by the Multiscope (after data quality review, some of the containers may fail and be removed from the dataset). Thus, each mouse can have one or more containers, each representing a unique imaging plane (experiment) that has been targeted across multiple recording sessions, under different behavioral and sensory conditions (session types).\\\\n21:The Change Detection task is a go/no-go task wherein mice are presented with a continuous series of flashed stimuli and they earn water rewards by correctly reporting when the identity of the flashed image changes.\\\\n24:Overview: Mice were trained for 1 hour/day, 5 days/week using a behavioral program implementing a go/no-go change detection task schematized in Figure 1. Briefly, mice were trained to lick a reward spout when the identity of a flashed visual stimulus changed identity. If mice responded correctly within a short, post-change response window (150-750ms before compensating for monitor display lag) a water reward was delivered. The volume of contingent rewards was 10uL in Stages 1 and 2, and reduced to 7uL after the first 3 sessions of Stage 3. The four stages of the training protocol are shown in Table 1\\\\n26:On Day 1 of the automated training protocol (shown in Figure 3) mice received a short, 15-min \\\"open loop\\\" session during which non-contingent water rewards were delivered coincident with 90\\u00b0 changes in orientation of a full-field, static square-wave grating (Stage 0). This session was intended to 1) introduce the mouse to the fluid delivery system and, 2) provide the technician an opportunity to identify the optimal lick spout position for each mouse. Each session thereafter was run in \\\"closed loop\\\", and progressed through 3 stages of the operant task: 1) static, full-field square wave gratings (oriented at 0\\u00b0 and 90\\u00b0, with the black/white transition always centered on the screen and the phase chosen randomly on every trial), 2) flashed, full-field square-wave gratings (0\\u00b0 and 90\\u00b0, with phase as described in 1), and 3) flashed full-field natural scenes (8 natural images used in the Allen Brain Observatory, http://observatory.brain-map.org/visualcoding). Once in the final stage of the task, mice were introduced to the additional session components that would be included during imaging sessions (5 min of gray screen immediately before and after the behavior session, as well as the final 5 min movie).\\\\n28:Progression through training stages: Starting with Stage 1, the advancement criteria required mice to achieve a session maximum performance of at least d-prime=2 (calculated over a rolling 100 trial window without trial count correction) during two of the last 3 sessions. The fastest progression from Stage 1 to Stage 3 was four training days.\\\\n30:Behavior session and trial structure: Each behavior session consisted of a continuous series of trials, schematized below.\\\\n32:Prior to the start of each trial a trial-type and change-time were selected. Trial-type was chosen based on predetermined frequencies such that \\\"GO\\\" and \\\"CATCH\\\" trials occurred with specified probabilities. In stages 1 and 2, the catch probability was ~36%. In stages 3 and above, the catch probability was initially ~30%. However, later sessions implemented a matrix sampling algorithm that ensured that each image transition was sampled equally, pushing the actual catch probability to ~12.5%. Change-times were selected from a truncated exponential distribution ranging from 2.25 to 8.25 seconds (mean of 4.25 seconds) following the start of a trial. Due to computational lag when aligning change-time with a stimulus flash, the actual distribution of change times was shifted to the right by one 750ms flash cycle (with only a small fraction of changes occurring at 2.25 seconds) resulting in a mean change time of 4.2 seconds. In trials when a mouse licked prior to the stimulus change the trial was reset, and a timeout period was imposed. The number of times a trial could be reset before re-drawing the timing parameter was limited to five. In all, this trial structure leads to a sampling of \\\"GO\\\" and \\\"CATCH\\\" trials, that when combined with mouse responding, yields \\\"HIT\\\", \\\"MISS\\\", \\\"FALSE ALARM\\\", and \\\"CORRECT REJECTION\\\" trials.\\\\n34:In addition to the four trial types described above, behavior sessions contained a subset of \\\"free reward\\\" trials (\\\"GO\\\" trials followed immediately by delivery of a non-contingent reward). Behavior sessions across all phases began with 5 \\\"free-reward\\\" trials. Additionally, in order to promote continued task performance throughout the behavior session \\\"free reward\\\" trials were delivered after 10 consecutive \\\"MISS\\\" trials. All non-contingent rewards were 5uL in volume.\\\\n38:2. At least 100 HIT and/or MISS trials on three consecutive sessions\\\\n42:Hit and false alarm rates: The hit rate was calculated as the fraction of go-trials in which the mouse licked in a 0.150 to 0.750 second window following the non-display-lag-compensated image display time. On Catch trials, a response window was defined as a 0.150 to 0.750 second window following the sham change. False alarm rates were calculated as the fraction of catch-trials in which animal emitted a lick in this response window. Reaction times were recalculated post-hoc after accounting for the calculated display lag, which ranged from ~20-35 ms. Due to variability in the display lag calculations, the minimum and maximum reaction times that resulted in trial classification (hit/miss/false-alarm/correct-rejection) will vary from session to session.\\\\n44:The AllenSDK returns hit rates, false alarm rates, and d-prime calculated over a rolling 100 trial window, excluding aborted trials (trials where the animal responded before the stimulus change). The reward rate is calculated over a 25 trial rolling window and provides a measure of the rewards earned per unit time (in units of rewards/minute). This provides a rough proxy for task engagement, with periods of high reward rates indicating engagement in the visual task. Low reward rates can indicate either disengagement (lack of responses), or extremely high rates of anticipatory licking (thereby continually delaying stimulus changes). In cases where the SDK has labeled epochs as either \\\\'engaged\\\\' or \\\\'disengaged\\\\', this corresponds to reward rates above and below 2 rewards per minute.\\\\n46:The animal\\\\'s running speed was monitored using an analog encoder mounted at the center of the 6.5\\\" (16.5 cm) diameter flat running wheel (see section APPARATUS for details). The encoder was provided with a continuous 5V reference voltage (v_in). A reference signal (v_sig), along with the actual value of v_in, was read from the encoder at discrete sampling intervals and logged to the data file. The ratio of v_sig to v_in corresponded to the position of the encoder relative to a zero-point. For example, v_sig/v_in = 0 corresponds to 0 degrees, v_sig/v_in = 0.5 corresponds to 180 degrees, etc. As the encoder crossed its zero-point (v_sig/v_in = 1.0), v_sig returned to 0. The rotational speed of the encoder was then accessible as the change in position since the last encoder measurement divided by the time interval since the last measurement (i.e., the derivative). The rotational speed was translated to a linear speed under the assumption that the mouse was running at a location 2/3 of the distance from the wheel center.\\\\n48:In practice, there were a few sources of error in the recordings including inconsistencies in the timing of the analog channel reads, spurious transients on the analog input channels, and inconsistencies in the derivative as the encoder crossed the zero-point. The following processing steps were followed to provide an estimate of the running speed despite these issues. All code for encoder processing and running speed calculation is visible in the brain_observatory.behavior.running_processing module of the AllenSDK. The value of v_sig was first \\\\'unwrapped\\\\', to account for the crossings of the zero-point in the encoder and facilitate the derivate step to follow. This was accomplished by first removing any voltage values greater than 5.1 (to remove spurious transients), then identifying and compensating for the zero-crossing points (or \\\\'wraps\\\\').\\\\n50:The voltage was converted to angular speed, in radians/s, by differentiating the unwrapped v_sig values, dividing by v_in, then multiplying by 2*pi. Angular speed was converted to linear speed using the above-described assumption of the animal\\\\'s running position on the wheel as follows:\\\\n51:running_speed_cm_per_sec = angular_speed * (2/3 * wheel_radius)\\\\n53:Remaining transients at the wrap points were identified and removed by limiting the values at the wrap points to be within the range defined by all other data points in a +/- 0.25 second window. Any additional transients with z-score of 10 or greater were removed (set to NaN). Finally, inconsistencies in the analog channel read times were accounted for by smoothing the raw running speed with a 10 Hz lowpass Butterworth filter. Both the unfiltered (but wrap and transient corrected) and the filtered running speeds are available to end users as, respectively, the `running_speed_raw` and `running_speed` attributes. Below is the linear speed, in cm/s, for the same 10 second period shown above in Figure 5:\\\\n56:Temporal synchronization of all data-streams (calcium imaging, visual stimulation, body and eye tracking cameras) was achieved by recording all experimental clocks on a single NI PCI-6612 digital IO board at 100 kHz (Figure 4). All input lines on the synchronization board were sampled simultaneously as a single 32-bit integer at 100 kHz (hardware-timed). To mitigate data size after collection, only changing events and their corresponding sample numbers were stored on a dedicated HDF5 file along with additional metadata.\\\\n60:Each experimental session was for data integrity based on a broad range of operational parameters. A comprehensive report was automatically generated to track data trends, animal behavior, experimental failures and errors. To minimize bias, rig operators did not review their own experiments, a secondary operator was assigned to review the QC report.\\\\n62:Assessment of the following quality metrics was performed after each imaging session. Failure to meet any of these criteria resulted in the session being retaken on a subsequent day.\\\\n66:4. Z-Axis Stability: Stability of image recording was assessed by comparing a windowed average image from the first and last 5 minutes of the experiment to a z-stack of images (+/- 30 \\u03bcm around imaging site, 0.75 \\u03bcm step) collected at the end of each experimental session to calculate the amount of drift that occurred over the session. Experiments with z-drift above 10\\u03bcm over the course of the entire session were excluded.\\\\n67:5. Animal Stress: Behavior videos were viewed to confirm that animals did not show excessive signs of stress. Any animal that showed eye secretion covering the pupil or excessive orbital tightening was returned to its home cage to recover. The presence of nose bulge, flailing and abnormal postures was also monitored.\\\\n71:9. Excessive Motion: Imaging frames were checked for residual motion (after motion correction algorithms had been applied)\\\\n72:10. Interictal Events: Presence of interictal events was assessed by measuring the full field fluorescence and calculating the intensity spike prominence and width for the first 10,000 frames of the 2P movie. Experiments with a non-zero probability of interictal events were then checked manually in order to exclude any potentially epileptic mice (see Steinmetz et al., 2017).\\\\n82:To account for variation in scanning rate across each line in the 2p-imaging frame, due to the non-constant speed of the resonant scanner, data acquired with the Scientifica microscopes required a dewarping step (Multiscope instruments have built-in dewarping correction). Correcting for the warping in the image involves taking a subset of the warped image\\\\'s columns, towards the edges of the image where the scanning speed is lower, and combining them using parameters derived from calibration data acquired with a standard grid image. To determine correct dewarping parameters, the grid image is adjusted until the dewarped grid is uniform. Each side of the image is dewarped independently.\\\\n84:The following equations are used to determine which columns are chosen from the warped image and how they are combined. The jth column of the dewarped image will be given by the formula:\\\\n85:col[j] = (1 + \\u230ag[j \\u2212 1]\\u230b \\u2212 g[j \\u2212 1]) * input_image[\\u230ag[j \\u2212 1]\\u230b] + (g[j] \\u2212 \\u230ag[j]\\u230b) * input_image[\\u230ag[j]\\u230b]),\\\\n90:Notice that this is taking a linear combination of the two (generally, adjacent) columns of the original image, using the decimal parts of the g function as coefficients. If these ever end up being non-adjacent columns from the original image, then we also add the value of the skipped column to col[j]. In the event that both f[j] and g[j - 1] are not nonnegative, then we fill that column with the average from the original image. And if f is nonnegative but g is not, then we simply replace that column with the exact same column from the original image.\\\\n92:For each experiment, there are four parameters that tell us how much the image has been warped. These are called aL, aR, bL, and bR. The L and R refer to the side of the image which is being dewarped. In the equations above, a and b are replaced with their corresponding parameter, depending on whether you are dewarping the left side or the right side. The parameters aL and aR specify how far (in pixels) into the image the warping occurs. The parameters bL and bR are more a measure of how warped the image is in those areas.\\\\n95:We used Suite2P v0.9.3 rigid registration for motion correction of 2-photon movies. Suite2P performs an iterative phase-correlation-based registration on a small subset of frames to generate a reference image from the average projection of those frames. The registration of the entire movie then proceeds with registration by phase correlation of each frame to this reference image. We saved Suite2P\\\\'s registered output tiff stacks and concatenated them into a single hdf5 format compatible with the rest of our processing pipeline.\\\\n97:Suite2P\\\\'s parameter maxregshift has a default value of 0.1, which clips lateral motions of more than 10% of the FOV dimension. We found a few examples in our release data where our experiments had real, long-timescale lateral shifts of greater than 10%. We increased the maxregshift parameter to 0.2, to allow for registration of these experiments. A consequence of this change was that up to 20% lateral shifts were allowed by Suite2P over short timescales as well. We observed that these short timescale shifts, often single frames, were likely not physical shifts, but struggles of the registration algorithm to register low signal-to-noise frames. Across an entire experiment, we monitor the worst-case shifts and establish a motion border which invalidates any ROI which touches it. To prevent too much inflation of the motion border exclusion area by these unphysical short timescale shifts, we detrended the x and y lateral corrections with a median filter over a 3 second window. We clip frame displacements to a +/-5% window around any outlier more than 5% above or below the detrended corrections was truncated to 5%. This allowed us to handle physical long timescale drifts and limit the impact of artifactual short timescale shifts. Any movie frames where the lateral shift was truncated were translated to the new truncated translation value.\\\\n103:The Visual Behavior 2P project used the same segmentation procedure that was developed for the Visual Coding 2P dataset, published in de Vries et al., 2020. The active cell segmentation module was designed to locate active cells within a field-of-\\u20267657 tokens ```dff_traces``` but the output provides traces of extrapolated events. Events are computed on unmixed dff traces for each cell as described in [Giovannucci et al. 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30652683/). The magnitude of events approximates the firing rate of neurons with the resolusion of about 200 ms. The biggest advantage of using events over dff traces is they exclude prolonged Ca transients that may conteminate neural responses to subsequent stimuli. You can also use ```filtered_events``` which are events convolved with a filter created using ```stats.halfnorm``` method. \\\\ntutorials/visual_behavior_load_ophys_data.py:218:# ```lambda``` is computed from Poisson distribution of events in the trace (think of it as a center of mass of the distribution, larger lambda == higher \\\"firing rate\\\").\\\\ntutorials/visual_behavior_load_ophys_data.py:220:# ```noise_std``` is a measure of variability in the events trace.\\\\ntutorials/visual_behavior_load_ophys_data.py:225:# The timestamps are the same for ```dff_traces``` and ```events```, in seconds\\\\ntutorials/visual_behavior_load_ophys_data.py:228:dataset.ophys_timestamps\\\\ntutorials/visual_behavior_load_ophys_data.py:233:# We can select a random cell from the experiment and plot its dff and events traces along with other behavioral and stimulus data.\\\\ntutorials/visual_behavior_load_ophys_data.py:236:cell_specimen_ids = dataset.cell_specimen_table.index.values # Check same number of trials across neural/input/output\\\\n        if len(data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session]) != ntrials:\\\\n            errors.append(f\\\"Session {session}: neural has {ntrials} trials but input has {len(data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session])}\\\")\\\\n        if len(data[\\\\'output\\\\'][session]) != ntrials:\\\\n            errors.append(f\\\"Session {session}: neural has {ntrials} trials but output has {len(data[\\\\'output\\\\'][session])}\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        if ntrials == 0:\\\\n            warnings.append(f\\\"Session {session} has 0 trials\\\")\\\\n            continue\\\\n\\\\n        # Check each trial\\\\n        for trial in range(ntrials):\\\\n\\\\n            # Check that each trial is a numpy array\\\\n            if not isinstance(data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][session][trial], np.ndarray):\\\\n                errors.append(f\\\"Session {session}, trial {trial}: neural must be numpy array, got {type(data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][session][trial])}\\\")\\\\n                continue\\\\n            if not isinstance(data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial], np.ndarray):\\\\n                errors.append(f\\\"Session {session}, trial {trial}: input must be numpy array, got {type(data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial])}\\\")\\\\n                continue\\\\n            if not isinstance(d\\u2026719 tokens truncated\\u2026rial.dtype}, expected int or float\\\")\\\\n\\\\n            #  errors.append(\\\"Length of output_values does not match doutput\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    valid = len(errors) == 0\\\\n    return valid, errors, warnings\\\\n\\\\ndef get_dim(x: list):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    Get the 0th dimension from the data.\\\\n    Inputs:\\\\n        x: list of length nsessions, x[session] is a list of length ntrials[session], and x[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (d, T)\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        d: int, dimension\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    ntrials_per_session = [len(x[session]) for session in range(len(x))]\\\\n    session = np.nonzero(ntrials_per_session)[0][0]\\\\n    d = x[session][0].shape[0]\\\\n    return d\\\\n    return d\\\\n\\\\ndef get_range(x: list):\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n    Get the range of values across all sessions and trials.\\\\n    Inputs:\\\\n        x: list of length nsessions, x[session] is a list of length ntrials[session], and x[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (d, T)\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        min_x: numpy array of shape (dinput,), minimum input values\\\\n        max_x: numpy array of shape (dinput,), maximum input values\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n\\\\n    d = get_dim(x)\\\\n    min_x = np.zeros(d) + np.inf\\\\n    max_x = np.zeros(d) - np.inf\\\\n    for session in range(len(x)):\\\\n        for trial in range(len(x[session])):\\\\n            input_trial = x[session][trial]\\\\n            if np.ndim(input_trial) == 1:\\\\n                min_x = np.minimum(min_x, input_trial)\\\\n                max_x = np.maximum(max_x, input_trial)\\\\n            else:\\\\n                min_x = np.minimum(min_x, input_trial.min(axis=1))\\\\n                max_x = np.maximum(max_x, input_trial.max(axis=1))\\\\n    return min_x, max_x\\\\n\\\\ndef print(f\\\"Number of sessions: {nsessions}\\\")\\\\n    ntrials_per_session = [len(data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][session]) for session in range(nsessions)]\\\\n    print(f\\\"Total number of trials: data[\\\\'output\\\\'][session][0].shape[0]    \\\\n    print(f\\\"Input dimension: {dinput}\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\"Output dimension: {doutput}\\\")\\\\n    \\\\n    mean_T = []\\\\n    nneurons =  ntrials = len(data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][session])\\\\n        Ts = [data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][session][trial].shape[1] for trial in range(ntrials)]\\\\n        nneurons_curr =     np.max([data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial] for trial in range(ntrials)],axis=0)))\\\\n        else:\\\\n            input_range.append((np.min([data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial].min(axis=1)        np.max([data[\\\\'output\\\\'][session][trial] for trial in range(ntrials)],axis=0)))\\\\n        else:\\\\n            output_range.append((np.min([data[\\\\'output\\\\'][session][trial].min(axis=1) for trial in range(ntrials)],axis=0),\\\\n a specific trial of a specific session. If predictions are provided,\\\\n    plot them alongside the true output.\\\\n    Inputs:\\\\n        data: dict with keys \\\\'neural\\\\', \\\\'input\\\\', \\\\'output\\\\' as described in train_decoder.py\\\\n        session: index of the session to plot, default = 0\\\\n        trial: index of the trial to plot, default = 0\\\\n        ax: optional list of matplotlib axes to plot on. integer, if provided, will sample this many neurons for plotting.\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        fig: matplotlib figure\\\\n        ax: list of matplotlib axes\\\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\\\n\\\\n    if ax is ax[0].plot(data[\\\\'neural\\\\'][session][trial][neuron,:]/maxv+i)\\\\n    ax[0].set_ylim((0,len(neuronstoplot)))\\\\n    ax[0].set_ylabel(\\\\'Neural\\\\')\\\\n\\\\n    dinput = np.ndim(data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial]) == 1:\\\\n        input = np.tile(data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial][:, np.newaxis], (1, T))\\\\n    else:\\\\n        input = data[\\\\'input\\\\'][session][trial]\\\\n    for indim in range(dinput):\\\\n        # Normalize to [0, 1] range for plotting\\\\n        z = (max_input[indim] - min_input[indim])\\\\n
